⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$N = P - Z \implies Z = P - N \implies Z = P - N_{ccw}; \quad \text{Closed-loop stability requires } Z = 0 \implies N_{ccw} = P$$

Nyquist stability theorem maps s-plane contour into $G(s)H(s)$ plane. The number of counter-clockwise encirclements of $(-1, j0)$ must equal the number of open-loop RHP poles $P$ for closed-loop stability.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The Nyquist stability criterion, formulated by Harry Nyquist in 1932, is a powerful frequency-domain technique that determines the closed-loop stability of dynamic systems directly from the open-loop frequency response $G(j\omega)H(j\omega)$ without computing closed-loop roots. It accommodates non-minimum phase plants and systems with pure transport time delays.

The mathematical foundation rests on Cauchy's Principle of the Argument from complex variable theory: Let $F(s)$ be a single-valued analytic function with $Z$ zeros and $P$ poles inside a closed continuous contour $\Gamma_s$ traversed in the clockwise direction in the s-plane. As $s$ traverses $\Gamma_s$ once in the clockwise direction, the corresponding locus of $F(s)$ in the complex $F(s)$-plane encircles the origin $N$ times in the clockwise direction, where: $$N = Z - P$$ If $P > Z$, $N$ is negative, indicating $|N|$ counter-clockwise encirclements of the origin.

In closed-loop control systems, the characteristic function is: $$F(s) = 1 + G(s)H(s)$$ Notice that: 1. The poles of $F(s)$ are identical to the poles of $G(s)H(s)$ (the open-loop poles). 2. The zeros of $F(s) = 1 + G(s)H(s) = 0$ are the closed-loop poles. Instead of plotting $1 + G(s)H(s)$ around the origin, we subtract $1$ and plot the open-loop transfer function $G(s)H(s)$ directly. Encirclements of the origin by $1 + G(s)H(s)$ correspond precisely to encirclements of the critical point $(-1 + j0)$ by the locus of $G(s)H(s)$.

Nyquist Contour: To detect whether any closed-loop poles reside in the unstable right half of the s-plane (RHP), we construct the Nyquist Contour $\Gamma_s$ enclosing the entire right-half s-plane: 1. Path along the imaginary axis from $s = -j\infty$ to $+j\infty$. (If open-loop poles exist at the origin, small semicircular indentations of radius $\epsilon \to 0$ bypass the poles into the RHP). 2. A large semicircular arc $s = R e^{j\theta}$ as $R \to \infty$ traversing from $\theta = +90^\circ$ through $0^\circ$ to $-90^\circ$.

The Nyquist Stability Theorem: Let: - $P$ = number of open-loop poles of $G(s)H(s)$ lying strictly in the right half of the s-plane (RHP). - $Z$ = number of closed-loop poles lying in the right half of the s-plane. - $N$ = number of clockwise encirclements of the critical point $(-1 + j0)$ by the complete Nyquist plot of $G(s)H(s)$ as $s$ traverses the Nyquist contour. Then: $$N = P - Z \implies Z = P - N$$ For a closed-loop system to be strictly stable, it must possess absolutely zero closed-loop poles in the right half plane ($Z = 0$). Therefore, the necessary and sufficient condition for closed-loop stability is: $$N = P \quad \text{or} \quad N_{ccw} = P$$ where $N_{ccw} = -N$ is the number of counter-clockwise encirclements of $(-1 + j0)$. - If the open-loop system is already stable ($P = 0$), then stability requires $N = 0$; that is, the Nyquist plot must not encircle the critical point $(-1 + j0)$ at all. - If the open-loop system is open-loop unstable with $P$ poles in the RHP, then for closed-loop stability, the Nyquist plot must encircle the critical point $(-1 + j0)$ exactly $P$ times in the counter-clockwise direction ($N = -P$).

Step-by-Step Stability Analysis of an Open-Loop Unstable System: Consider an open-loop unstable plant: $$G(s)H(s) = \frac{K(s + 2)}{(s - 1)(s + 4)}$$ Here, there is an open-loop pole in the RHP at $s = +1$. Therefore, $P = 1$. For the closed-loop system to be stable, we require $Z = 0$, which mandates: $$N = P - Z = 1 - 0 = +1 \quad (\text{meaning } 1 \text{ counter-clockwise encirclement of } -1+j0, N_{ccw} = 1)$$ Evaluating $G(j\omega)H(j\omega)$ along the imaginary axis $s = j\omega$: $$G(j\omega)H(j\omega) = \frac{K(j\omega + 2)}{(j\omega - 1)(j\omega + 4)}$$ At $\omega = 0$: $$G(0)H(0) = \frac{K(2)}{(-1)(4)} = -\frac{K}{2}$$ The low-frequency intercept lies on the negative real axis at $-\frac{K}{2}$. As $\omega \to \infty$: $$G(j\omega)H(j\omega) \to \frac{K j\omega}{(j\omega)^2} = \frac{K}{j\omega} \to 0 \angle -90^\circ$$ The locus terminates at the origin from the fourth quadrant. The real-axis intercept at $\omega = 0$ is $-\frac{K}{2}$. If $-\frac{K}{2} < -1 \implies K > 2$, the point $(-1 + j0)$ falls inside the loop formed by the Nyquist locus and its infinite semicircle, resulting in exactly one counter-clockwise encirclement ($N_{ccw} = 1$). Under this condition: $$Z = P - N_{ccw} = 1 - 1 = 0$$ Thus, for $K > 2$, the closed-loop system is completely stable! If $K < 2$, the critical point $(-1 + j0)$ is not encircled ($N = 0$), which gives $Z = P - 0 = 1$, leaving one closed-loop pole in the RHP and causing instability. This elegant result demonstrates how feedback with sufficient gain can stabilize an inherently open-loop unstable dynamic plant.

A large semicircular arc $s = R e^{j\theta}$ as $R \to \infty$ traversing from $\theta = +90^\circ$ through $0^\circ$ to $-90^\circ$.

Step-by-Step Stability Analysis of an Open-Loop Unstable System: Consider an open-loop unstable plant: $$G(s)H(s) = \frac{K(s + 2)}{(s - 1)(s + 4)}$$ Here, there is an open-loop pole in the RHP at $s = +1$. Therefore, $P = 1$. For the closed-loop system to be stable, we require $Z = 0$, which mandates: $$N = P - Z = 1 - 0 = +1 \quad (\text{meaning } 1 \text{ counter-clockwise encirclement of } -1+j0, N_{ccw} = 1)$$ Evaluating $G(j\omega)H(j\omega)$ along the imaginary axis $s = j\omega$: $$G(j\omega)H(j\omega) = \frac{K(j\omega + 2)}{(j\omega - 1)(j\omega + 4)}$$ At $\omega = 0$: $$G(0)H(0) = \frac{K(2)}{(-1)(4)} = -\frac{K}{2}$$ The low-frequency intercept lies on the negative real axis at $-\frac{K}{2}$. As $\omega \to \infty$: $$G(j\omega)H(j\omega) \to \frac{K j\omega}{(j\omega)^2} = \frac{K}{j\omega} \to 0 \angle -90^\circ$$ The locus terminates at the origin from the fourth quadrant. The real-axis intercept at $\omega = 0$ is $-\frac{K}{2}$. If $-\frac{K}{2} < -1 \implies K > 2$, the point $(-1 + j0)$ falls inside the loop formed by the Nyquist locus and its infinite semicircle, resulting in exactly one counter-clockwise encirclement ($N_{ccw} = 1$). If $K < 2$, the critical point $(-1 + j0)$ is not encircled ($N = 0$), which gives $Z = P - 0 = 1$, leaving one closed-loop pole in the RHP and causing instability. This elegant result demonstrates how feedback with sufficient gain can stabilize an inherently open-loop unstable dynamic plant.

Module 5

Compensator Design and State Space Basics

6 Elaborated University Questions & Answers

3 Marks (Short Answer)
Short Answer ID: LCS-Q21

Explain the circuits, transfer functions, and pole-zero patterns of phase lead, phase lag, and lead-lag compensator networks. Derive the maximum phase lead angle phi_m and frequency omega_m for a lead network. Explain the circuits, transfer functions, and pole-zero patterns of phase lead, phase lag, and lead-lag compensator networks. Derive the maximum phase lead angle phi_m and frequency omega_m for a lead network. Explain the circuits, transfer functions, and pole-zero patterns of phase lead, phase lag, and lead-lag compensator networks. Derive the maximum phase lead angle phi_m and frequency omega_m for a lead network.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$G_{lead}(s) = \frac{s + 1/T}{s + 1/(\alpha T)}, \quad \sin\phi_m = \frac{1-\alpha}{1+\alpha}, \quad \omega_m = \frac{1}{T\sqrt{\alpha}}, \quad 0 < \alpha < 1$$

Lead compensators place zero closer to origin than pole ($\alpha < 1$), providing maximum phase lead $\phi_m = \sin^{-1}(\frac{1-\alpha}{1+\alpha})$ at geometric mean frequency $\omega_m = 1/(T\sqrt{\alpha})$ to boost bandwidth and damping.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$G_{lead}(s) = \frac{s + 1/T}{s + 1/(\alpha T)}, \quad \sin\phi_m = \frac{1-\alpha}{1+\alpha}, \quad \omega_m = \frac{1}{T\sqrt{\alpha}}, \quad 0 < \alpha < 1$$

Lead compensators place zero closer to origin than pole ($\alpha < 1$), providing maximum phase lead $\phi_m = \sin^{-1}(\frac{1-\alpha}{1+\alpha})$ at geometric mean frequency $\omega_m = 1/(T\sqrt{\alpha})$ to boost bandwidth and damping.

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$G_{lead}(s) = \frac{s + 1/T}{s + 1/(\alpha T)}, \quad \sin\phi_m = \frac{1-\alpha}{1+\alpha}, \quad \omega_m = \frac{1}{T\sqrt{\alpha}}, \quad 0 < \alpha < 1$$

Lead compensators place zero closer to origin than pole ($\alpha < 1$), providing maximum phase lead $\phi_m = \sin^{-1}(\frac{1-\alpha}{1+\alpha})$ at geometric mean frequency $\omega_m = 1/(T\sqrt{\alpha})$ to boost bandwidth and damping.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

In feedback control system design, simple gain adjustments frequently cannot satisfy both steady-state accuracy and transient response specifications simultaneously. Compensators are auxiliary physical hardware networks or digital filter algorithms introduced into the control loop to reshape the overall frequency response and open-loop pole-zero pattern.

1. Phase Lead Compensator: A passive phase-lead network consists of a parallel combination of resistor $R_1$ and capacitor $C$, connected in series with a resistor $R_2$. The voltage transfer function is derived by voltage division: $$G_c(s) = \frac{V_o(s)}{V_i(s)} = \frac{R_2}{R_2 + \frac{R_1 \cdot \frac{1}{sC}}{R_1 + \frac{1}{sC}}} = \frac{R_2(1 + s R_1 C)}{R_2(1 + s R_1 C) + R_1} = \frac{R_2(1 + s R_1 C)}{(R_1 + R_2) + s R_1 R_2 C}$$ Factoring out $(R_1 + R_2)$: $$G_c(s) = \frac{R_2}{R_1 + R_2} \cdot \frac{1 + s R_1 C}{1 + s \left(\frac{R_1 R_2}{R_1 + R_2}\right) C}$$ Defining the time constant $T = R_1 C$ and the attenuation factor $\alpha = \frac{R_2}{R_1 + R_2} < 1$: $$G_c(s) = \alpha \cdot \frac{1 + sT}{1 + s \alpha T} = \frac{s + \frac{1}{T}}{s + \frac{1}{\alpha T}}$$ Since $\alpha < 1$, the zero at $s = -1/T$ is closer to the origin than the pole at $s = -1/(\alpha T)$. Substituting $s = j\omega$, the phase angle is: $$\phi(\omega) = \tan^{-1}(\omega T) - \tan^{-1}(\alpha \omega T)$$ Because $\alpha < 1$, $\phi(\omega) > 0$ for all $\omega > 0$, generating positive phase lead. To find the frequency $\omega_m$ where the maximum phase lead $\phi_m$ occurs, we maximize $\tan\phi$: $$\tan\phi = \frac{\omega T - \alpha \omega T}{1 + \alpha \omega^2 T^2} = \frac{(1 - \alpha)\omega T}{1 + \alpha \omega^2 T^2}$$ Differentiating with respect to $\omega$ and setting the numerator derivative to zero: $$\frac{d}{d\omega} \left[\frac{\omega}{1 + \alpha \omega^2 T^2}\right] = \frac{(1 + \alpha \omega^2 T^2)(1) - \omega(2\alpha \omega T^2)}{(1 + \alpha \omega^2 T^2)^2} = \frac{1 - \alpha \omega^2 T^2}{(1 + \alpha \omega^2 T^2)^2} = 0$$ $$1 - \alpha \omega_m^2 T^2 = 0 \implies \omega_m = \frac{1}{T \sqrt{\alpha}}$$ The frequency $\omega_m$ is the geometric mean of the corner frequencies: $\omega_m = \sqrt{\frac{1}{T} \cdot \frac{1}{\alpha T}}$. Substituting $\omega_m = \frac{1}{T\sqrt{\alpha}}$ back into the expression for $\tan\phi_m$: $$\tan\phi_m = \frac{(1 - \alpha) \frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}}}{1 + \alpha \left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)} = \frac{1 - \alpha}{2\sqrt{\alpha}}$$ Constructing a right-angled triangle with opposite side $(1 - \alpha)$ and adjacent side $2\sqrt{\alpha}$, the hypotenuse is $\sqrt{(1 - \alpha)^2 + 4\alpha} = \sqrt{1 - 2\alpha + \alpha^2 + 4\alpha} = \sqrt{(1 + \alpha)^2} = 1 + \alpha$. Thus, the maximum phase lead angle is given by the celebrated relation: $$\sin\phi_m = \frac{1 - \alpha}{1 + \alpha} \iff \alpha = \frac{1 - \sin\phi_m}{1 + \sin\phi_m}$$ The magnitude at $\omega_m$ is $|G_c(j\omega_m)| = \sqrt{\alpha}$, corresponding to a attenuation of $20\log_{10}\sqrt{\alpha} = -10\log_{10}(1/\alpha) \text{ dB}$.

2. Phase Lag Compensator: A passive phase-lag network swaps the placement: a series combination of $R_1$ and $C$ connects in parallel with $R_2$ across the output terminals. Its transfer function is: $$G_c(s) = \frac{s + \frac{1}{T}}{s + \frac{1}{\beta T}} = \frac{1 + sT}{1 + s\beta T}, \quad \text{where } \beta > 1$$ In a lag compensator, the pole at $s = -1/(\beta T)$ is located much closer to the origin than the zero at $s = -1/T$. It introduces negative phase lag at intermediate frequencies while providing high attenuation (gain reduction by $20\log_{10}\beta \text{ dB}$) at high frequencies.

Comparison of Compensators: - Lead Compensator: Increases system bandwidth, speeds up transient response, improves damping ratio $\zeta$ and phase margin. Trade-off: Amplifies high-frequency noise and increases maximum actuator effort. - Lag Compensator: Drastically suppresses high-frequency loop gain, permitting high open-loop gain to minimize steady-state error without destabilizing the system. Trade-off: Reduces closed-loop bandwidth and slows down transient response. - Lead-Lag Compensator: Combines both networks in cascade ($G_c(s) = \frac{s + 1/T_1}{s + 1/(\alpha T_1)} \cdot \frac{s + 1/T_2}{s + 1/(\beta T_2)}$ with $\alpha < 1, \beta > 1$), simultaneously providing high steady-state precision and fast transient damping.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

In feedback control system design, simple gain adjustments frequently cannot satisfy both steady-state accuracy and transient response specifications simultaneously. Compensators are auxiliary physical hardware networks or digital filter algorithms introduced into the control loop to reshape the overall frequency response and open-loop pole-zero pattern.

1. Phase Lead Compensator: A passive phase-lead network consists of a parallel combination of resistor $R_1$ and capacitor $C$, connected in series with a resistor $R_2$. The voltage transfer function is derived by voltage division: $$G_c(s) = \frac{V_o(s)}{V_i(s)} = \frac{R_2}{R_2 + \frac{R_1 \cdot \frac{1}{sC}}{R_1 + \frac{1}{sC}}} = \frac{R_2(1 + s R_1 C)}{R_2(1 + s R_1 C) + R_1} = \frac{R_2(1 + s R_1 C)}{(R_1 + R_2) + s R_1 R_2 C}$$ Factoring out $(R_1 + R_2)$: $$G_c(s) = \frac{R_2}{R_1 + R_2} \cdot \frac{1 + s R_1 C}{1 + s \left(\frac{R_1 R_2}{R_1 + R_2}\right) C}$$ Defining the time constant $T = R_1 C$ and the attenuation factor $\alpha = \frac{R_2}{R_1 + R_2} < 1$: $$G_c(s) = \alpha \cdot \frac{1 + sT}{1 + s \alpha T} = \frac{s + \frac{1}{T}}{s + \frac{1}{\alpha T}}$$ Since $\alpha < 1$, the zero at $s = -1/T$ is closer to the origin than the pole at $s = -1/(\alpha T)$. Substituting $s = j\omega$, the phase angle is: $$\phi(\omega) = \tan^{-1}(\omega T) - \tan^{-1}(\alpha \omega T)$$ Because $\alpha < 1$, $\phi(\omega) > 0$ for all $\omega > 0$, generating positive phase lead. To find the frequency $\omega_m$ where the maximum phase lead $\phi_m$ occurs, we maximize $\tan\phi$: $$\tan\phi = \frac{\omega T - \alpha \omega T}{1 + \alpha \omega^2 T^2} = \frac{(1 - \alpha)\omega T}{1 + \alpha \omega^2 T^2}$$ Differentiating with respect to $\omega$ and setting the numerator derivative to zero: $$\frac{d}{d\omega} \left[\frac{\omega}{1 + \alpha \omega^2 T^2}\right] = \frac{(1 + \alpha \omega^2 T^2)(1) - \omega(2\alpha \omega T^2)}{(1 + \alpha \omega^2 T^2)^2} = \frac{1 - \alpha \omega^2 T^2}{(1 + \alpha \omega^2 T^2)^2} = 0$$ $$1 - \alpha \omega_m^2 T^2 = 0 \implies \omega_m = \frac{1}{T \sqrt{\alpha}}$$ The frequency $\omega_m$ is the geometric mean of the corner frequencies: $\omega_m = \sqrt{\frac{1}{T} \cdot \frac{1}{\alpha T}}$. Substituting $\omega_m = \frac{1}{T\sqrt{\alpha}}$ back into the expression for $\tan\phi_m$: $$\tan\phi_m = \frac{(1 - \alpha) \frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}}}{1 + \alpha \left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)} = \frac{1 - \alpha}{2\sqrt{\alpha}}$$ Constructing a right-angled triangle with opposite side $(1 - \alpha)$ and adjacent side $2\sqrt{\alpha}$, the hypotenuse is $\sqrt{(1 - \alpha)^2 + 4\alpha} = \sqrt{1 - 2\alpha + \alpha^2 + 4\alpha} = \sqrt{(1 + \alpha)^2} = 1 + \alpha$. Thus, the maximum phase lead angle is given by the celebrated relation: $$\sin\phi_m = \frac{1 - \alpha}{1 + \alpha} \iff \alpha = \frac{1 - \sin\phi_m}{1 + \sin\phi_m}$$ The magnitude at $\omega_m$ is $|G_c(j\omega_m)| = \sqrt{\alpha}$, corresponding to a attenuation of $20\log_{10}\sqrt{\alpha} = -10\log_{10}(1/\alpha) \text{ dB}$.

2. Phase Lag Compensator: A passive phase-lag network swaps the placement: a series combination of $R_1$ and $C$ connects in parallel with $R_2$ across the output terminals. Its transfer function is: $$G_c(s) = \frac{s + \frac{1}{T}}{s + \frac{1}{\beta T}} = \frac{1 + sT}{1 + s\beta T}, \quad \text{where } \beta > 1$$ In a lag compensator, the pole at $s = -1/(\beta T)$ is located much closer to the origin than the zero at $s = -1/T$. It introduces negative phase lag at intermediate frequencies while providing high attenuation (gain reduction by $20\log_{10}\beta \text{ dB}$) at high frequencies.

Comparison of Compensators: - Lead Compensator: Increases system bandwidth, speeds up transient response, improves damping ratio $\zeta$ and phase margin. Trade-off: Amplifies high-frequency noise and increases maximum actuator effort. - Lag Compensator: Drastically suppresses high-frequency loop gain, permitting high open-loop gain to minimize steady-state error without destabilizing the system. Trade-off: Reduces closed-loop bandwidth and slows down transient response. - Lead-Lag Compensator: Combines both networks in cascade ($G_c(s) = \frac{s + 1/T_1}{s + 1/(\alpha T_1)} \cdot \frac{s + 1/T_2}{s + 1/(\beta T_2)}$ with $\alpha < 1, \beta > 1$), simultaneously providing high steady-state precision and fast transient damping.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

In feedback control system design, simple gain adjustments frequently cannot satisfy both steady-state accuracy and transient response specifications simultaneously. Compensators are auxiliary physical hardware networks or digital filter algorithms introduced into the control loop to reshape the overall frequency response and open-loop pole-zero pattern.

1. Phase Lead Compensator: A passive phase-lead network consists of a parallel combination of resistor $R_1$ and capacitor $C$, connected in series with a resistor $R_2$. The voltage transfer function is derived by voltage division: $$G_c(s) = \frac{V_o(s)}{V_i(s)} = \frac{R_2}{R_2 + \frac{R_1 \cdot \frac{1}{sC}}{R_1 + \frac{1}{sC}}} = \frac{R_2(1 + s R_1 C)}{R_2(1 + s R_1 C) + R_1} = \frac{R_2(1 + s R_1 C)}{(R_1 + R_2) + s R_1 R_2 C}$$ Factoring out $(R_1 + R_2)$: $$G_c(s) = \frac{R_2}{R_1 + R_2} \cdot \frac{1 + s R_1 C}{1 + s \left(\frac{R_1 R_2}{R_1 + R_2}\right) C}$$ Defining the time constant $T = R_1 C$ and the attenuation factor $\alpha = \frac{R_2}{R_1 + R_2} < 1$: $$G_c(s) = \alpha \cdot \frac{1 + sT}{1 + s \alpha T} = \frac{s + \frac{1}{T}}{s + \frac{1}{\alpha T}}$$ Since $\alpha < 1$, the zero at $s = -1/T$ is closer to the origin than the pole at $s = -1/(\alpha T)$. Substituting $\omega_m = \frac{1}{T\sqrt{\alpha}}$ back into the expression for $\tan\phi_m$: $$\tan\phi_m = \frac{(1 - \alpha) \frac{1}{\sqrt{\alpha}}}{1 + \alpha \left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\right)} = \frac{1 - \alpha}{2\sqrt{\alpha}}$$ Constructing a right-angled triangle with opposite side $(1 - \alpha)$ and adjacent side $2\sqrt{\alpha}$, the hypotenuse is $\sqrt{(1 - \alpha)^2 + 4\alpha} = \sqrt{1 - 2\alpha + \alpha^2 + 4\alpha} = \sqrt{(1 + \alpha)^2} = 1 + \alpha$. Thus, the maximum phase lead angle is given by the celebrated relation: $$\sin\phi_m = \frac{1 - \alpha}{1 + \alpha} \iff \alpha = \frac{1 - \sin\phi_m}{1 + \sin\phi_m}$$ The magnitude at $\omega_m$ is $|G_c(j\omega_m)| = \sqrt{\alpha}$, corresponding to a attenuation of $20\log_{10}\sqrt{\alpha} = -10\log_{10}(1/\alpha) \text{ dB}$.

2. Phase Lag Compensator: A passive phase-lag network swaps the placement: a series combination of $R_1$ and $C$ connects in parallel with $R_2$ across the output terminals. Its transfer function is: $$G_c(s) = \frac{s + \frac{1}{T}}{s + \frac{1}{\beta T}} = \frac{1 + sT}{1 + s\beta T}, \quad \text{where } \beta > 1$$ In a lag compensator, the pole at $s = -1/(\beta T)$ is located much closer to the origin than the zero at $s = -1/T$. It introduces negative phase lag at intermediate frequencies while providing high attenuation (gain reduction by $20\log_{10}\beta \text{ dB}$) at high frequencies.

Comparison of Compensators: - Lead Compensator: Increases system bandwidth, speeds up transient response, improves damping ratio $\zeta$ and phase margin. Trade-off: Amplifies high-frequency noise and increases maximum actuator effort. - Lag Compensator: Drastically suppresses high-frequency loop gain, permitting high open-loop gain to minimize steady-state error without destabilizing the system. Trade-off: Reduces closed-loop bandwidth and slows down transient response. - Lead-Lag Compensator: Combines both networks in cascade ($G_c(s) = \frac{s + 1/T_1}{s + 1/(\alpha T_1)} \cdot \frac{s + 1/T_2}{s + 1/(\beta T_2)}$ with $\alpha < 1, \beta > 1$), simultaneously providing high steady-state precision and fast transient damping.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

The frequency-domain synthesis of a phase lead compensator reshapes the open-loop frequency response to satisfy simultaneous requirements on steady-state accuracy (such as static velocity error constant $K_v$) and relative stability (phase margin $PM$).

Consider the standard lead compensator transfer function: $$G_c(s) = \frac{s + \frac{1}{T}}{s + \frac{1}{\alpha T}} = \frac{1 + sT}{1 + s\alpha T}, \quad 0 < \alpha < 1$$ Because the DC gain of this network as $s \to 0$ is $1$, introducing the compensator does not alter the static error constants if the loop gain is maintained.

Step-by-Step Algorithmic Design Procedure: Step 1: Determine the Open-Loop Gain $K$: Evaluate the required open-loop amplifier gain $K$ to satisfy the specified steady-state error or error constant. For instance, if a velocity error constant $K_v$ is specified: $$K_v = \lim_{s \to 0} s G(s) \implies K = \text{evaluated value}$$ Define the uncompensated system transfer function with gain $K$ incorporated as $G_1(s) = K G(s)$.

Step 2: Plot the Bode Diagram of the Uncompensated System: Construct the asymptotic magnitude plot and phase plot of $G_1(j\omega)$. From this plot, determine: - The uncompensated gain crossover frequency $\omega_{gc}$. - The uncompensated phase margin $\gamma_{un} = 180^\circ + \angle G_1(j\omega_{gc})$.

Step 3: Calculate the Required Phase Lead $\phi_m$: Let $\gamma_{spec}$ denote the desired closed-loop phase margin specified by the designer. Adding the lead compensator introduces phase lead, but its high-frequency gain will shift the gain crossover frequency to a higher value where the plant has greater phase lag. To counteract this destabilizing shift, an empirical safety margin $\epsilon \approx 5^\circ \text{ to } 8^\circ$ is added: $$\phi_m = \gamma_{spec} - \gamma_{un} + \epsilon$$

Step 4: Determine the Attenuation Factor $\alpha$: Using the derived trigonometric relation: $$\alpha = \frac{1 - \sin\phi_m}{1 + \sin\phi_m}$$ Note: If $\phi_m > 60^\circ$, a single-stage lead network yields an excessively small $\alpha$, causing severe noise amplification. In such cases, two identical cascaded lead networks each providing $\phi_m / 2$ should be designed.

Step 5: Identify the New Gain Crossover Frequency $\omega'_{gc}$: The lead compensator introduces a magnitude boost of $+10 \log_{10}(1/\alpha) \text{ dB}$ at its maximum phase frequency $\omega_m = 1/(T\sqrt{\alpha})$. To make $\omega_m$ coincide exactly with the new gain crossover frequency $\omega'_{gc}$, locate the frequency on the uncompensated magnitude curve where: $$20 \log_{10}|G_1(j\omega'_{gc})| = -10 \log_{10}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha}\right) = 20\log_{10}\sqrt{\alpha} \text{ dB}$$ Read this frequency $\omega'_{gc}$ directly from the Bode magnitude plot and assign: $$\omega_m = \omega'_{gc}$$

Step 6: Calculate the Compensator Corner Frequencies: Using $\omega_m = \frac{1}{T\sqrt{\alpha}}$: - Time constant: $T = \frac{1}{\omega_m \sqrt{\alpha}}$ - Zero corner frequency: $\omega_z = \frac{1}{T} = \omega_m \sqrt{\alpha}$ - Pole corner frequency: $\omega_p = \frac{1}{\alpha T} = \frac{\omega_m}{\sqrt{\alpha}}$

Step 7: Formulate the Final Compensated Transfer Function: The complete compensated open-loop transfer function is: $$G_{open}(s) = G_c(s) G_1(s) = \left(\frac{1 + sT}{1 + s\alpha T}\right) G_1(s)$$

Step 8: Verification: Re-evaluate the phase margin of the compensated system at $\omega'_{gc}$: $$\gamma_{final} = 180^\circ + \angle G_c(j\omega'_{gc}) + \angle G_1(j\omega'_{gc})$$ If $\gamma_{final} \ge \gamma_{spec}$, the design is complete. If the achieved phase margin is slightly below specifications due to steep plant phase roll-off, increase the safety margin $\epsilon$ by $2^\circ \text{ to } 3^\circ$ and repeat from Step 3.

Lag compensator design identifies frequency $\omega'_{gc}$ where plant phase provides required margin $+ \epsilon$, attenuates gain by $\beta$, and places zero one decade below $\omega'_{gc}$ to minimize phase degradation.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

A phase lag compensator is employed when the uncompensated control system exhibits acceptable transient response characteristics but suffers from unacceptably large steady-state tracking errors. Rather than relying on phase addition, the lag compensator achieves stability by attenuating high-frequency loop gain, lowering the gain crossover frequency to a lower frequency where the uncompensated plant possesses an inherently healthy phase margin.

The transfer function of the phase lag compensator is universally expressed in time-constant form as: $$G_c(s) = \beta \cdot \frac{1 + sT}{1 + s\beta T} = \frac{s + \frac{1}{T}}{s + \frac{1}{\beta T}}, \quad \beta > 1$$ At zero frequency ($s \to 0$), the gain is $\beta$, which amplifies the low-frequency loop gain to satisfy steady-state error constants. At high frequencies ($\omega \gg 1/T$), the high-frequency attenuation is $\frac{1}{\beta}$, shifting the magnitude curve downward by $-20\log_{10}\beta \text{ dB}$.

Step-by-Step Algorithmic Design Procedure: Step 1: Evaluate the Loop Gain $K$: Determine the amplifier gain $K$ necessary to satisfy the specified steady-state error constant (e.g., $K_v$ or $K_p$). Incorporate this gain into the uncompensated system: $$G_1(s) = K G(s)$$

Step 2: Construct the Bode Plot of the Gain-Adjusted System: Plot the magnitude and phase curves of $G_1(j\omega)$. Evaluate the uncompensated phase margin $\gamma_{un}$. Typically, due to the high gain $K$, the uncompensated system exhibits an unacceptably small or negative phase margin.

Step 3: Select the New Gain Crossover Frequency $\omega'_{gc}$: Let $\gamma_{spec}$ represent the required phase margin. Because the lag compensator contributes a small residual phase lag at high frequencies, we allocate a safety margin $\epsilon \approx 5^\circ \text{ to } 12^\circ$ (commonly $5^\circ$). Find the frequency on the uncompensated phase curve where the phase angle equals: $$\phi_{plant}(\omega'_{gc}) = -180^\circ + \gamma_{spec} + \epsilon$$ Read this specific frequency directly from the phase plot and designate it as the new gain crossover frequency $\omega'_{gc}$.

Step 4: Determine the Lag Factor $\beta$: At the newly chosen frequency $\omega'_{gc}$, the compensated loop magnitude must equal $0 \text{ dB}$. Thus, the lag compensator must provide an exact high-frequency attenuation equal to the excess gain of $G_1(j\omega'_{gc})$: $$20 \log_{10}|G_1(j\omega'_{gc})| = 20 \log_{10}\beta \text{ dB}$$ $$\beta = 10^{\frac{|G_1(j\omega'_{gc})|_{\text{dB}}}{20}} = |G_1(j\omega'_{gc})|$$

Step 5: Place the Compensator Zero and Pole: To prevent the phase lag of the compensator from eroding the phase margin at the new crossover frequency $\omega'_{gc}$, the compensator zero must be placed well below $\omega'_{gc}$, typically one decade below: $$\omega_z = \frac{1}{T} = 0.1 \times \omega'_{gc} = \frac{\omega'_{gc}}{10}$$ Consequently: $$T = \frac{10}{\omega'_{gc}}$$ The compensator pole is positioned at: $$\omega_p = \frac{1}{\beta T} = \frac{\omega_z}{\beta} = \frac{\omega'_{gc}}{10 \beta}$$ At $\omega = \omega'_{gc}$, the phase contribution of the lag compensator is: $$\phi_c(\omega'_{gc}) = \tan^{-1}(\omega'_{gc} T) - \tan^{-1}(\beta \omega'_{gc} T) = \tan^{-1}(10) - \tan^{-1}(10\beta) \approx 84.3^\circ - 89.5^\circ \approx -5.2^\circ$$ This validates the chosen safety margin $\epsilon \approx 5^\circ$.

Step 6: Assemble the Compensated System Transfer Function: $$G_{open}(s) = \left(\frac{1 + sT}{1 + s\beta T}\right) G_1(s)$$

Step 7: Verification: Re-plot the compensated Bode magnitude and phase curves and verify that the actual phase margin satisfies $\gamma_{final} \ge \gamma_{spec}$ while the steady-state error constant remains precisely at the specified target.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

A phase lag compensator is employed when the uncompensated control system exhibits acceptable transient response characteristics but suffers from unacceptably large steady-state tracking errors. Rather than relying on phase addition, the lag compensator achieves stability by attenuating high-frequency loop gain, lowering the gain crossover frequency to a lower frequency where the uncompensated plant possesses an inherently healthy phase margin.

The transfer function of the phase lag compensator is universally expressed in time-constant form as: $$G_c(s) = \beta \cdot \frac{1 + sT}{1 + s\beta T} = \frac{s + \frac{1}{T}}{s + \frac{1}{\beta T}}, \quad \beta > 1$$ At zero frequency ($s \to 0$), the gain is $\beta$, which amplifies the low-frequency loop gain to satisfy steady-state error constants. At high frequencies ($\omega \gg 1/T$), the high-frequency attenuation is $\frac{1}{\beta}$, shifting the magnitude curve downward by $-20\log_{10}\beta \text{ dB}$.

Step-by-Step Algorithmic Design Procedure: Step 1: Evaluate the Loop Gain $K$: Determine the amplifier gain $K$ necessary to satisfy the specified steady-state error constant (e.g., $K_v$ or $K_p$). Incorporate this gain into the uncompensated system: $$G_1(s) = K G(s)$$

Step 2: Construct the Bode Plot of the Gain-Adjusted System: Plot the magnitude and phase curves of $G_1(j\omega)$. Evaluate the uncompensated phase margin $\gamma_{un}$. Typically, due to the high gain $K$, the uncompensated system exhibits an unacceptably small or negative phase margin.

Step 3: Select the New Gain Crossover Frequency $\omega'_{gc}$: Let $\gamma_{spec}$ represent the required phase margin. Because the lag compensator contributes a small residual phase lag at high frequencies, we allocate a safety margin $\epsilon \approx 5^\circ \text{ to } 12^\circ$ (commonly $5^\circ$). Find the frequency on the uncompensated phase curve where the phase angle equals: $$\phi_{plant}(\omega'_{gc}) = -180^\circ + \gamma_{spec} + \epsilon$$ Read this specific frequency directly from the phase plot and designate it as the new gain crossover frequency $\omega'_{gc}$.

Step 4: Determine the Lag Factor $\beta$: At the newly chosen frequency $\omega'_{gc}$, the compensated loop magnitude must equal $0 \text{ dB}$. Thus, the lag compensator must provide an exact high-frequency attenuation equal to the excess gain of $G_1(j\omega'_{gc})$: $$20 \log_{10}|G_1(j\omega'_{gc})| = 20 \log_{10}\beta \text{ dB}$$ $$\beta = 10^{\frac{|G_1(j\omega'_{gc})|_{\text{dB}}}{20}} = |G_1(j\omega'_{gc})|$$

Step 5: Place the Compensator Zero and Pole: To prevent the phase lag of the compensator from eroding the phase margin at the new crossover frequency $\omega'_{gc}$, the compensator zero must be placed well below $\omega'_{gc}$, typically one decade below: $$\omega_z = \frac{1}{T} = 0.1 \times \omega'_{gc} = \frac{\omega'_{gc}}{10}$$ Consequently: $$T = \frac{10}{\omega'_{gc}}$$ The compensator pole is positioned at: $$\omega_p = \frac{1}{\beta T} = \frac{\omega_z}{\beta} = \frac{\omega'_{gc}}{10 \beta}$$ At $\omega = \omega'_{gc}$, the phase contribution of the lag compensator is: $$\phi_c(\omega'_{gc}) = \tan^{-1}(\omega'_{gc} T) - \tan^{-1}(\beta \omega'_{gc} T) = \tan^{-1}(10) - \tan^{-1}(10\beta) \approx 84.3^\circ - 89.5^\circ \approx -5.2^\circ$$ This validates the chosen safety margin $\epsilon \approx 5^\circ$.

Step 6: Assemble the Compensated System Transfer Function: $$G_{open}(s) = \left(\frac{1 + sT}{1 + s\beta T}\right) G_1(s)$$

Step 7: Verification: Re-plot the compensated Bode magnitude and phase curves and verify that the actual phase margin satisfies $\gamma_{final} \ge \gamma_{spec}$ while the steady-state error constant remains precisely at the specified target.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

Explain the state-space representation of continuous-time LTI systems. Explain the state-space representation of continuous-time LTI systems. Define state, state variables, state vector, and state equations, and state the major advantages of state space analysis over transfer functions. Explain the state-space representation of continuous-time LTI systems. Define state, state variables, state vector, and state equations, and state the major advantages of state space analysis over transfer functions.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\dot{\mathbf{x}}(t) = A \mathbf{x}(t) + B \mathbf{u}(t), \quad \mathbf{y}(t) = C \mathbf{x}(t) + D \mathbf{u}(t), \quad \mathbf{x}(t) \in \mathbb{R}^n$$

State-space represents $n$-th order dynamic systems via coupled first-order vector differential equations, accommodating MIMO architectures, initial conditions, non-linearities, and internal state observability.

$$\dot{\mathbf{x}}(t) = A \mathbf{x}(t) + B \mathbf{u}(t), \quad \mathbf{y}(t) = C \mathbf{x}(t) + D \mathbf{u}(t), \quad \mathbf{x}(t) \in \mathbb{R}^n$$

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 5 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$\dot{\mathbf{x}}(t) = A \mathbf{x}(t) + B \mathbf{u}(t), \quad \mathbf{y}(t) = C \mathbf{x}(t) + D \mathbf{u}(t), \quad \mathbf{x}(t) \in \mathbb{R}^n$$

State-space represents $n$-th order dynamic systems via coupled first-order vector differential equations, accommodating MIMO architectures, initial conditions, non-linearities, and internal state observability.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

Classical control theory relies predominantly on input-output transfer functions in the complex frequency domain. While highly effective for single-input single-output (SISO) linear time-invariant systems with zero initial conditions, transfer function techniques treat the plant as a 'black box' and obscure the internal energy distribution. Modern control theory overcomes these limitations through the state space approach.

The state of a dynamic system is defined as the minimal set of variables $x_1(t), x_2(t), \dots, x_n(t)$ such that the knowledge of these variables at an initial time $t = t_0$, together with the complete specification of the input vector $u(t)$ for $t \ge t_0$, completely determines the future state trajectory and output vector $y(t)$ for all $t > t_0$.

For an $n$-th order, $m$-input, $p$-output continuous-time linear time-invariant system, the state-space model is formulated by two vector-matrix equations: 1. State Differential Equation: $$\dot{x}(t) = A x(t) + B u(t)$$ 2. Output Algebraic Equation: $$y(t) = C x(t) + D u(t)$$ where: - $x(t) \in \mathbb{R}^n$ is the $n \times 1$ State Vector grouping all $n$ state variables. - $\dot{x}(t) \in \mathbb{R}^n$ is the state velocity vector $\left[\frac{dx_1}{dt}, \dots, \frac{dx_n}{dt}\right]^T$. - $u(t) \in \mathbb{R}^m$ is the $m \times 1$ Input (Control) Vector. - $y(t) \in \mathbb{R}^p$ is the $p \times 1$ Output Vector. - $A \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times n}$ is the System Matrix (governing internal state dynamics). - $B \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times m}$ is the Input Distribution Matrix. - $C \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times n}$ is the Output (Observation) Matrix. - $D \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times m}$ is the Direct Transmission (Feedthrough) Matrix (frequently zero in physical systems).

In the operational block diagram, the state vector $x(t)$ represents the physical outputs of a bank of $n$ parallel analog integrators ($1/s$). The vector $\dot{x}(t)$ drives the integrator inputs, synthesized by summing the linear feedback $A x(t)$ and the input excitation $B u(t)$.

Major Advantages of State Space Analysis over Transfer Functions: 1. MIMO Capability: State space natively handles Multiple-Input Multiple-Output (MIMO) systems without needing cumbersome matrices of cross-coupled transfer functions. 2. Initial Conditions: It naturally incorporates non-zero initial conditions $x(0) \ne 0$ into transient solutions. 3. Internal Visibility: It exposes internal system states (such as mechanical stresses or inductor currents), facilitating the detection of uncontrollable or unobservable dynamic modes that are invisible in transfer functions due to pole-zero cancellations. 4. Non-Linear and Time-Varying Systems: The state-space framework extends directly to non-linear differential equations ($\dot{x} = f(x, u, t)$) and time-varying systems where Laplace transforms cannot be applied. 5. Optimal and Digital Control: It provides the computational foundation for modern state feedback pole placement, Kalman filtering, and linear quadratic regulator (LQR) design.

7/14 Marks (Long Essay / Derivation)
Long Derivation ID: LCS-Q25

Derive the closed-loop transfer function matrix G(s) = C(sI - A)^{-1}B + D from state space equations. Prove that the poles of G(s) are the eigenvalues of state matrix A and solve a numerical problem. Derive the closed-loop transfer function matrix G(s) = C(sI - A)^{-1}B + D from state space equations. Prove that the poles of G(s) are the eigenvalues of state matrix A and solve a numerical problem. Derive the closed-loop transfer function matrix G(s) = C(sI - A)^{-1}B + D from state space equations. Prove that the poles of G(s) are the eigenvalues of state matrix A and solve a numerical problem.

Watch Video Solution
⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$G(s) = C (s I - A)^{-1} B + D = \frac{C \operatorname{adj}(s I - A) B}{\det(s I - A)} + D$$

Taking Laplace transforms with zero initial conditions relates state-space to transfer functions, proving that transfer function poles are identically the eigenvalues of state matrix $A$ ($\det(sI-A)=0$).

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$G(s) = C (s I - A)^{-1} B + D = \frac{C \operatorname{adj}(s I - A) B}{\det(s I - A)} + D$$

Taking Laplace transforms with zero initial conditions relates state-space to transfer functions, proving that transfer function poles are identically the eigenvalues of state matrix $A$ ($\det(sI-A)=0$).

⚡ Key Equation
⏱️ 12 Mins Writing Time ⭐ High Mark Weightage
$$G(s) = C (s I - A)^{-1} B + D = \frac{C \operatorname{adj}(s I - A) B}{\det(s I - A)} + D$$

Taking Laplace transforms with zero initial conditions relates state-space to transfer functions, proving that transfer function poles are identically the eigenvalues of state matrix $A$ ($\det(sI-A)=0$).

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

To establish the formal link between modern state space theory and classical frequency-domain techniques, we derive the overall transfer function matrix directly from the continuous state differential equations.

Consider the general LTI state-space model: $$\dot{x}(t) = A x(t) + B u(t)$$ $$y(t) = C x(t) + D u(t)$$ By definition, the transfer function is the ratio of the Laplace transform of the output to the Laplace transform of the input under the strict assumption of zero initial conditions ($x(0) = 0$).

Taking the unilateral Laplace transform on both sides of the state differential equation: $$\mathcal{L}\{\dot{x}(t)\} = s X(s) - x(0) = A X(s) + B U(s)$$ Substituting $x(0) = 0$: $$s X(s) = A X(s) + B U(s)$$ Collecting the state transform vectors on the left-hand side: $$s X(s) - A X(s) = B U(s)$$ $$(s I - A) X(s) = B U(s)$$ where $I$ is the $n \times n$ identity matrix, and $(sI - A)$ is the characteristic matrix. Provided $s$ does not coincide with any eigenvalue of $A$, the matrix $(sI - A)$ is invertible. Premultiplying both sides by the matrix inverse $(sI - A)^{-1}$: $$X(s) = (s I - A)^{-1} B U(s)$$ The matrix $\Phi(s) = (sI - A)^{-1}$ is known as the resolvent matrix.

Next, taking the Laplace transform of the output equation: $$Y(s) = C X(s) + D U(s)$$ Substituting the derived expression for $X(s)$ into the output equation: $$Y(s) = C \left[ (s I - A)^{-1} B U(s) \right] + D U(s)$$ Factoring out the input vector $U(s)$ on the right-hand side: $$Y(s) = \left[ C (s I - A)^{-1} B + D \right] U(s)$$ Dividing by $U(s)$ yields the celebrated transfer function matrix formula: $$G(s) = \frac{Y(s)}{U(s)} = C (s I - A)^{-1} B + D$$ Using the adjoint formulation for the matrix inverse: $$(s I - A)^{-1} = \frac{\text{adj}(s I - A)}{\det(s I - A)}$$ Substituting this gives: $$G(s) = \frac{C \, \text{adj}(s I - A) \, B}{\det(s I - A)} + D$$ This algebraic result proves that the poles of the transfer function $G(s)$ are precisely the roots of the characteristic equation $\det(sI - A) = 0$, meaning that system poles are identical to the eigenvalues of the state matrix $A$.

Comprehensive Numerical Demonstration from KTU PYQ: A system is described by the state space matrices: $$A = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -2 & -3 \end{bmatrix}, \quad B = \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{bmatrix}, \quad C = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}, \quad D = [0]$$ Determine the closed-loop transfer function $G(s) = \frac{Y(s)}{U(s)}$.

Step 1: Construct the matrix $(sI - A)$: $$s I - A = s \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} - \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -2 & -3 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} s & 0 \\ 0 & s \end{bmatrix} - \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -2 & -3 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} s & -1 \\ 2 & s + 3 \end{bmatrix}$$

Step 2: Calculate the determinant $\det(sI - A)$: $$\det(s I - A) = s(s + 3) - (-1)(2) = s^2 + 3s + 2 = (s + 1)(s + 2)$$ The system eigenvalues are $\lambda_1 = -1$ and $\lambda_2 = -2$.

Step 3: Evaluate the adjoint matrix $\text{adj}(sI - A)$: For a $2 \times 2$ matrix $\begin{bmatrix} a & b \\ c & d \end{bmatrix}$, the adjoint is $\begin{bmatrix} d & -b \\ -c & a \end{bmatrix}$: $$\text{adj}(s I - A) = \begin{bmatrix} s + 3 & 1 \\ -2 & s \end{bmatrix}$$

Step 4: Compute the matrix product $\text{adj}(sI - A) B$: $$\text{adj}(s I - A) B = \begin{bmatrix} s + 3 & 1 \\ -2 & s \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} (s+3)(0) + (1)(1) \\ (-2)(0) + (s)(1) \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ s \end{bmatrix}$$

Step 5: Compute the row-vector multiplication $C \left[\text{adj}(sI - A) B\right]$: $$C \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ s \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ s \end{bmatrix} = (1)(1) + (0)(s) = 1$$

Step 6: Assemble the transfer function $G(s)$: $$G(s) = \frac{1}{\det(s I - A)} + 0 = \frac{1}{s^2 + 3s + 2} = \frac{1}{(s + 1)(s + 2)}$$ The transfer function has poles at $s = -1$ and $s = -2$, confirming complete equivalence with the eigenvalues of matrix $A$.

🌴 Manglish Local Tone Study Guide

To establish the formal link between modern state space theory and classical frequency-domain techniques, we derive the overall transfer function matrix directly from the continuous state differential equations.

Consider the general LTI state-space model: $$\dot{x}(t) = A x(t) + B u(t)$$ $$y(t) = C x(t) + D u(t)$$ By definition, the transfer function is the ratio of the Laplace transform of the output to the Laplace transform of the input under the strict assumption of zero initial conditions ($x(0) = 0$).

Taking the unilateral Laplace transform on both sides of the state differential equation: $$\mathcal{L}\{\dot{x}(t)\} = s X(s) - x(0) = A X(s) + B U(s)$$ Substituting $x(0) = 0$: $$s X(s) = A X(s) + B U(s)$$ Collecting the state transform vectors on the left-hand side: $$s X(s) - A X(s) = B U(s)$$ $$(s I - A) X(s) = B U(s)$$ where $I$ is the $n \times n$ identity matrix, and $(sI - A)$ is the characteristic matrix. Provided $s$ does not coincide with any eigenvalue of $A$, the matrix $(sI - A)$ is invertible. Premultiplying both sides by the matrix inverse $(sI - A)^{-1}$: $$X(s) = (s I - A)^{-1} B U(s)$$ The matrix $\Phi(s) = (sI - A)^{-1}$ is known as the resolvent matrix.

Next, taking the Laplace transform of the output equation: $$Y(s) = C X(s) + D U(s)$$ Substituting the derived expression for $X(s)$ into the output equation: $$Y(s) = C \left[ (s I - A)^{-1} B U(s) \right] + D U(s)$$ Factoring out the input vector $U(s)$ on the right-hand side: $$Y(s) = \left[ C (s I - A)^{-1} B + D \right] U(s)$$ Dividing by $U(s)$ yields the celebrated transfer function matrix formula: $$G(s) = \frac{Y(s)}{U(s)} = C (s I - A)^{-1} B + D$$ Using the adjoint formulation for the matrix inverse: $$(s I - A)^{-1} = \frac{\text{adj}(s I - A)}{\det(s I - A)}$$ Substituting this gives: $$G(s) = \frac{C \, \text{adj}(s I - A) \, B}{\det(s I - A)} + D$$ This algebraic result proves that the poles of the transfer function $G(s)$ are precisely the roots of the characteristic equation $\det(sI - A) = 0$, meaning that system poles are identical to the eigenvalues of the state matrix $A$.

Comprehensive Numerical Demonstration from KTU PYQ: A system is described by the state space matrices: $$A = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -2 & -3 \end{bmatrix}, \quad B = \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{bmatrix}, \quad C = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}, \quad D = [0]$$ Determine the closed-loop transfer function $G(s) = \frac{Y(s)}{U(s)}$.

Step 1: Construct the matrix $(sI - A)$: $$s I - A = s \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} - \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -2 & -3 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} s & 0 \\ 0 & s \end{bmatrix} - \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -2 & -3 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} s & -1 \\ 2 & s + 3 \end{bmatrix}$$

Step 2: Calculate the determinant $\det(sI - A)$: $$\det(s I - A) = s(s + 3) - (-1)(2) = s^2 + 3s + 2 = (s + 1)(s + 2)$$ The system eigenvalues are $\lambda_1 = -1$ and $\lambda_2 = -2$.

Step 3: Evaluate the adjoint matrix $\text{adj}(sI - A)$: For a $2 \times 2$ matrix $\begin{bmatrix} a & b \\ c & d \end{bmatrix}$, the adjoint is $\begin{bmatrix} d & -b \\ -c & a \end{bmatrix}$: $$\text{adj}(s I - A) = \begin{bmatrix} s + 3 & 1 \\ -2 & s \end{bmatrix}$$

Step 4: Compute the matrix product $\text{adj}(sI - A) B$: $$\text{adj}(s I - A) B = \begin{bmatrix} s + 3 & 1 \\ -2 & s \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} (s+3)(0) + (1)(1) \\ (-2)(0) + (s)(1) \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ s \end{bmatrix}$$

Step 5: Compute the row-vector multiplication $C \left[\text{adj}(sI - A) B\right]$: $$C \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ s \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ s \end{bmatrix} = (1)(1) + (0)(s) = 1$$

Step 6: Assemble the transfer function $G(s)$: $$G(s) = \frac{1}{\det(s I - A)} + 0 = \frac{1}{s^2 + 3s + 2} = \frac{1}{(s + 1)(s + 2)}$$ The transfer function has poles at $s = -1$ and $s = -2$, confirming complete equivalence with the eigenvalues of matrix $A$.

🇮🇳 മലയാളം പ്രാദേശിക പഠന സഹായി

To establish the formal link between modern state space theory and classical frequency-domain techniques, we derive the overall transfer function matrix directly from the continuous state differential equations.

Consider the general LTI state-space model: $$\dot{x}(t) = A x(t) + B u(t)$$ $$y(t) = C x(t) + D u(t)$$ By definition, the transfer function is the ratio of the Laplace transform of the output to the Laplace transform of the input under the strict assumption of zero initial conditions ($x(0) = 0$).

Taking the unilateral Laplace transform on both sides of the state differential equation: $$\mathcal{L}\{\dot{x}(t)\} = s X(s) - x(0) = A X(s) + B U(s)$$ Substituting $x(0) = 0$: $$s X(s) = A X(s) + B U(s)$$ Collecting the state transform vectors on the left-hand side: $$s X(s) - A X(s) = B U(s)$$ $$(s I - A) X(s) = B U(s)$$ where $I$ is the $n \times n$ identity matrix, and $(sI - A)$ is the characteristic matrix. Provided $s$ does not coincide with any eigenvalue of $A$, the matrix $(sI - A)$ is invertible. Premultiplying both sides by the matrix inverse $(sI - A)^{-1}$: $$X(s) = (s I - A)^{-1} B U(s)$$ The matrix $\Phi(s) = (sI - A)^{-1}$ is known as the resolvent matrix.

Next, taking the Laplace transform of the output equation: $$Y(s) = C X(s) + D U(s)$$ Substituting the derived expression for $X(s)$ into the output equation: $$Y(s) = C \left[ (s I - A)^{-1} B U(s) \right] + D U(s)$$ Factoring out the input vector $U(s)$ on the right-hand side: $$Y(s) = \left[ C (s I - A)^{-1} B + D \right] U(s)$$ Dividing by $U(s)$ yields the celebrated transfer function matrix formula: $$G(s) = \frac{Y(s)}{U(s)} = C (s I - A)^{-1} B + D$$ Using the adjoint formulation for the matrix inverse: $$(s I - A)^{-1} = \frac{\text{adj}(s I - A)}{\det(s I - A)}$$ Substituting this gives: $$G(s) = \frac{C \, \text{adj}(s I - A) \, B}{\det(s I - A)} + D$$ This algebraic result proves that the poles of the transfer function $G(s)$ are precisely the roots of the characteristic equation $\det(sI - A) = 0$, meaning that system poles are identical to the eigenvalues of the state matrix $A$.

Comprehensive Numerical Demonstration from KTU PYQ: A system is described by the state space matrices: $$A = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -2 & -3 \end{bmatrix}, \quad B = \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{bmatrix}, \quad C = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}, \quad D = [0]$$ Determine the closed-loop transfer function $G(s) = \frac{Y(s)}{U(s)}$.

Step 1: Construct the matrix $(sI - A)$: $$s I - A = s \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} - \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -2 & -3 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} s & 0 \\ 0 & s \end{bmatrix} - \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -2 & -3 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} s & -1 \\ 2 & s + 3 \end{bmatrix}$$

Step 2: Calculate the determinant $\det(sI - A)$: $$\det(s I - A) = s(s + 3) - (-1)(2) = s^2 + 3s + 2 = (s + 1)(s + 2)$$ The system eigenvalues are $\lambda_1 = -1$ and $\lambda_2 = -2$.

Step 3: Evaluate the adjoint matrix $\text{adj}(sI - A)$: For a $2 \times 2$ matrix $\begin{bmatrix} a & b \\ c & d \end{bmatrix}$, the adjoint is $\begin{bmatrix} d & -b \\ -c & a \end{bmatrix}$: $$\text{adj}(s I - A) = \begin{bmatrix} s + 3 & 1 \\ -2 & s \end{bmatrix}$$

Step 4: Compute the matrix product $\text{adj}(sI - A) B$: $$\text{adj}(s I - A) B = \begin{bmatrix} s + 3 & 1 \\ -2 & s \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} (s+3)(0) + (1)(1) \\ (-2)(0) + (s)(1) \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ s \end{bmatrix}$$

Step 5: Compute the row-vector multiplication $C \left[\text{adj}(sI - A) B\right]$: $$C \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ s \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix} \begin{bmatrix} 1 \\ s \end{bmatrix} = (1)(1) + (0)(s) = 1$$

Step 6: Assemble the transfer function $G(s)$: $$G(s) = \frac{1}{\det(s I - A)} + 0 = \frac{1}{s^2 + 3s + 2} = \frac{1}{(s + 1)(s + 2)}$$ The transfer function has poles at $s = -1$ and $s = -2$, confirming complete equivalence with the eigenvalues of matrix $A$.

Section A Exam-Ready Answer

In modern state space analysis, the state transition matrix constitutes the fundamental mathematical operator governing the unforced free evolution of an autonomous dynamic system.

Consider the unforced homogeneous continuous-time state equation: $$\dot{x}(t) = A x(t), \quad x(0) = x_0$$ This is a first-order vector-matrix differential equation whose scalar counterpart $\dot{x} = a x$ has the familiar exponential solution $x(t) = e^{at} x(0)$. By direct mathematical analogy, the solution to the vector system is expressed as: $$x(t) = e^{At} x(0) = \Phi(t) x(0)$$ where $\Phi(t) = e^{At}$ is the State Transition Matrix.

Formal Definition: The state transition matrix $\Phi(t)$ is defined by the infinite Taylor matrix series expansion: $$\Phi(t) = e^{At} = I + A t + \frac{A^2 t^2}{2!} + \frac{A^3 t^3}{3!} + \dots = \sum_{k=0}^\infty \frac{A^k t^k}{k!}$$ Alternatively, taking the Laplace transform of $\dot{x}(t) = A x(t)$: $$s X(s) - x(0) = A X(s) \implies (s I - A) X(s) = x(0) \implies X(s) = (s I - A)^{-1} x(0)$$ Taking the inverse Laplace transform yields the closed-form operational definition: $$\Phi(t) = e^{At} = \mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ (s I - A)^{-1} \right\}$$

Five Essential Properties of the State Transition Matrix: Property 1 (Identity at zero time): $$\Phi(0) = e^{A(0)} = I$$ Proof: Substituting $t = 0$ into the series expansion: $\Phi(0) = I + 0 + 0 + \dots = I$.

Property 2 (Inverse and Time-Reversal): $$\Phi^{-1}(t) = \Phi(-t) = e^{-At}$$ Proof: $\Phi(t)\Phi(-t) = e^{At} e^{-At} = e^{A(t - t)} = e^0 = I$. Thus, the inverse of $\Phi(t)$ is obtained simply by replacing $t$ with $-t$.

Property 3 (Semi-group Law / Successive Transitions): $$\Phi(t_1 + t_2) = \Phi(t_1) \Phi(t_2) = \Phi(t_2) \Phi(t_1)$$ Proof: Transitioning from $t = 0$ to $t_1$ gives $x(t_1) = \Phi(t_1)x(0)$. Transitioning from $t_1$ to $(t_1 + t_2)$ gives $x(t_1 + t_2) = \Phi(t_2) x(t_1) = \Phi(t_2)\Phi(t_1)x(0)$. Direct transition from $0$ gives $x(t_1 + t_2) = \Phi(t_1 + t_2)x(0)$. Equating both yields $\Phi(t_1 + t_2) = \Phi(t_1)\Phi(t_2)$.

Property 4 (Power Property): $$[\Phi(t)]^k = \Phi(k t) \quad \text{for any integer } k$$ Proof: Applying Property 3 repeatedly $k$ times: $[\Phi(t)]^k = (e^{At})^k = e^{A(k t)} = \Phi(k t)$.

Property 5 (Time Derivative): $$\frac{d\Phi(t)}{dt} = A \Phi(t) = \Phi(t) A$$ Proof: Differentiating the series term by term: $$\frac{d}{dt}\left[I + A t + \frac{A^2 t^2}{2!} + \dots\right] = 0 + A + A^2 t + \frac{A^3 t^2}{2!} + \dots = A\left[I + A t + \frac{A^2 t^2}{2!} + \dots\right] = A \Phi(t)$$

Full Analytical Computation from KTU PYQ: Given the system matrix: $$A = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ -2 & -3 \end{bmatrix}$$ Compute the state transition matrix $\Phi(t) = e^{At}$ using the Laplace transform resolvent method.

Step 1: Compute $(sI - A)$: $$s I - A = \begin{bmatrix} s & -1 \\ 2 & s + 3 \end{bmatrix}$$

Step 2: Evaluate determinant and inverse $(sI - A)^{-1}$: $$\det(s I - A) = s(s + 3) + 2 = s^2 + 3s + 2 = (s + 1)(s + 2)$$ $$\text{adj}(s I - A) = \begin{bmatrix} s + 3 & 1 \\ -2 & s \end{bmatrix}$$ $$(s I - A)^{-1} = \frac{1}{(s + 1)(s + 2)} \begin{bmatrix} s + 3 & 1 \\ -2 & s \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} \frac{s + 3}{(s + 1)(s + 2)} & \frac{1}{(s + 1)(s + 2)} \\ \frac{-2}{(s + 1)(s + 2)} & \frac{s}{(s + 1)(s + 2)} \end{bmatrix}$$

Step 3: Perform partial fraction expansions for each matrix entry: 1. Entry $(1,1)$: $$\frac{s + 3}{(s + 1)(s + 2)} = \frac{A_1}{s + 1} + \frac{B_1}{s + 2} = \frac{-1 + 3}{(-1 + 2)(s + 1)} + \frac{-2 + 3}{(-2 + 1)(s + 2)} = \frac{2}{s + 1} - \frac{1}{s + 2}$$ 2. Entry $(1,2)$: $$\frac{1}{(s + 1)(s + 2)} = \frac{1}{s + 1} - \frac{1}{s + 2}$$ 3. Entry $(2,1)$: $$\frac{-2}{(s + 1)(s + 2)} = \frac{-2}{s + 1} + \frac{2}{s + 2}$$ 4. Entry $(2,2)$: $$\frac{s}{(s + 1)(s + 2)} = \frac{-1}{s + 1} + \frac{2}{s + 2}$$

Step 4: Take inverse Laplace transforms term-by-term: $$\Phi(t) = \mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ (s I - A)^{-1} \right\} = \begin{bmatrix} 2 e^{-t} - e^{-2t} & e^{-t} - e^{-2t} \\ -2 e^{-t} + 2 e^{-2t} & -e^{-t} + 2 e^{-2t} \end{bmatrix}$$

Step 5: Verify Property 1 ($\Phi(0) = I$): $$\Phi(0) = \begin{bmatrix} 2(1) - 1 & 1 - 1 \\ -2(1) + 2(1) & -(1) + 2(1) \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} = I$$ The result perfectly matches the identity matrix, confirming the mathematical accuracy of the derived state transition matrix.

Property 2 (Inverse and Time-Reversal): $$\Phi^{-1}(t) = \Phi(-t) = e^{-At}$$ Proof: $\Phi(t)\Phi(-t) = e^{At} e^{-At} = e^{A(t - t)} = e^0 = I$. Thus, the inverse of $\Phi(t)$ is obtained simply by replacing $t$ with $-t$.

Property 3 (Semi-group Law / Successive Transitions): $$\Phi(t_1 + t_2) = \Phi(t_1) \Phi(t_2) = \Phi(t_2) \Phi(t_1)$$ Proof: Transitioning from $t = 0$ to $t_1$ gives $x(t_1) = \Phi(t_1)x(0)$. Equating both yields $\Phi(t_1 + t_2) = \Phi(t_1)\Phi(t_2)$.

Property 4 (Power Property): $$[\Phi(t)]^k = \Phi(k t) \quad \text{for any integer } k$$ Proof: Applying Property 3 repeatedly $k$ times: $[\Phi(t)]^k = (e^{At})^k = e^{A(k t)} = \Phi(k t)$.

Property 5 (Time Derivative): $$\frac{d\Phi(t)}{dt} = A \Phi(t) = \Phi(t) A$$ Proof: Differentiating the series term by term: $$\frac{d}{dt}\left[I + A t + \frac{A^2 t^2}{2!} + \dots\right] = 0 + A + A^2 t + \frac{A^3 t^2}{2!} + \dots = A\left[I + A t + \frac{A^2 t^2}{2!} + \dots\right] = A \Phi(t)$$

Step 3: Perform partial fraction expansions for each matrix entry: 1. Entry $(1,1)$: $$\frac{s + 3}{(s + 1)(s + 2)} = \frac{A_1}{s + 1} + \frac{B_1}{s + 2} = \frac{-1 + 3}{(-1 + 2)(s + 1)} + \frac{-2 + 3}{(-2 + 1)(s + 2)} = \frac{2}{s + 1} - \frac{1}{s + 2}$$ 2. Entry $(1,2)$: $$\frac{1}{(s + 1)(s + 2)} = \frac{1}{s + 1} - \frac{1}{s + 2}$$ 3. Entry $(2,1)$: $$\frac{-2}{(s + 1)(s + 2)} = \frac{-2}{s + 1} + \frac{2}{s + 2}$$ 4. Entry $(2,2)$: $$\frac{s}{(s + 1)(s + 2)} = \frac{-1}{s + 1} + \frac{2}{s + 2}$$

Step 4: Take inverse Laplace transforms term-by-term: $$\Phi(t) = \mathcal{L}^{-1}\left\{ (s I - A)^{-1} \right\} = \begin{bmatrix} 2 e^{-t} - e^{-2t} & e^{-t} - e^{-2t} \\ -2 e^{-t} + 2 e^{-2t} & -e^{-t} + 2 e^{-2t} \end{bmatrix}$$

Step 5: Verify Property 1 ($\Phi(0) = I$): $$\Phi(0) = \begin{bmatrix} 2(1) - 1 & 1 - 1 \\ -2(1) + 2(1) & -(1) + 2(1) \end{bmatrix} = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix} = I$$ The result perfectly matches the identity matrix, confirming the mathematical accuracy of the derived state transition matrix.

ℹ️ Schematic: Figure 5.6: State Transition Mapping Trajectory from Initial State x(0) to x(t) — (Refer to textbook/lecture notes)
